INTRODUCTION
SKETCH OF EARLY CARTOGRAPHY
The earliest may of St. Croix, now possessed by the United State8 Coast and
@odetic Survey, is a copy of the old French map engraved by Lapointe in 1071,
entitled "L'Isle de Ste. Crolx, situ& i 18 degr. 48 minutes de lat. Septentrionalle. Gouvern6e depuis 12 Ans par Monsieur du Bois." . The " plantages "
(estates), abandoned 26 years later, together with the original names of the
coaetal features, and their known or conjectural location, are herein listed.
Gerard van Keulen at Amsterdam, Holland, revised and reprinted Lapolnte's
map with French names and Dutch notes. Van Keulen also in 1719 published
an original chart of St. Thomas, in Dutch throughout; and this, notwithstauding disproportional drawing, exhibits a surprising amount of valid Information,
including the autlwtic spelling of many otherwise perplexing place names
which have came down to our time. His charta are contained in Voogt's
Zeefakkel, part 4.
British cartographers are represented, 20 years later, by C. Mortimer of
London, who compiled, from surveys by Joaeph Atkinson and others, his
'' Hydrographical Ohart of the Virgin Zslande."
About 1750 Dutch surveyors completed a cadastral survey of St. Thomas,
each sheet belng about a foot square and showing an estate of about 160
acme. The set is believed to be in private possession at Copenhagen.
The 5rst reliable chorographic map of St. Croix, exhlblting the 8ubdivisions
into quarters and eatates, which have continued with little change until the
present time. is the "Tilforladelig Kort over Eylandet St. Crolx," drawn by
I. M. Beck, and engraved by 0. H. de Lode a t Copenhagen in 1754. The
tracts are properly numbered, the proprietors' names added in legible script,
and each proprfetor's holdings distinctively hand tinted. The Library of Congreen has a prized original ; this bureau a clear photographic reproduction.
''LePetit Atlas Maritime" wan published in 6 volumes by Jacques Nicolas
Beltin at Parir in 1768. Volume 1 contains "Curte des Isles de Viergea," with
maps of St. Crolx and St. Thomas, showing the early French flace names.
Paul KUffner of Nnremberg in 1767 engraved German maps of St. Thomas
"mit den mehresten Plantagen" (with most of the plantations), and of St.
CroIx "mit den Namen der Plantagen die bestllndlg slnd" (with the names
of-the still exlstent plantations) ; most of the Eastend estates having been
meanwhile abandoned. Both of KUfPner's maps are in Oldendorp's MisslonsGlerrhicBte, and are so cited.

Charts of the Virgin Islands, from English and Danish sources, were pub.
iished in 1775 and subsequently by the English cartographer, Thomas Jefferya
Successive revisions appear in Purdy, Dessiou & Jefferys' West-India Atlas,
published by J. Whittle and R. H. Laurie, London, 1816-18.
Between 1780 and 1799 Peter Lotharius Oxholm, the accomplished cartographer, later a general and governor of the islands, aurveyed and m p m St.
John and St. Croix. His drawfngs were engraved by G. L. Angelo ht 'Copenhagen. The planters had already chrishned their estates with the fanciful
names which have since been perpetuated. Laurie & Whittle, the London
niap publishers, printed a n English edition of Oxholm's St. Croix, noteworthy
as having been reproduced by chart makers for 120 years, until replaced by
the recent work of the Coast and Geodetic Survey.
I n St. Thomas Colonial Council Hall, there hung, supposedly from Oxholm's
time till the American annexation, a very large framed map of the island.
A photographic copy was made by Henry Haswl, of St. Kitts, and this rephotographed for the writer by the courtesy of Mi.. Axel Holst, of St. Thomds.
The original is now loat.
The first original nautical chart of merit was that compiled by Capt. L. I.
Rohde in 1822,showing a hydrographic survey of the sobth side of St. Thomas.
Prof. H. B. Hornbeck's survey, made in 1835-1889, engraved at the Danish
Sgkortarkiv in 1846, superseded older maps of St. Thomas.
In 1891, Lieut. G. B. Lawrance, surveyor and cartographer, in rommand of
M. M. S. Bcorpim, made a thorough a n a comprehensive survey of all the watera
.:idjacent to the islandd, with incidental topography and mnoranlic SketcheR.
This survey became the basis for subsequent Admiralty charta. The Coast and
Geodetic Survey possesws facsimiles of the original drawings.
I n latter years, t h e Danish training ship IngoEf was accustomed to make
hydrographic surveys on her blennlal practfce cruises, supplementing the compilations on British Admiralty Charts, from which the Danish, French,
Spanish, and American charts derive most of their hydrography, while thelr
topography and noniendature a r e largely taken from the '' mother maps''
Secondary maps in books of history and travel, geographical descriptions,
treatises on geology, botany, and other sciences, and in missionary atlases,
owe their value to t h e critical scholarship of the compflers.
It Is rather remarkable t h a t nearly all the maps of these Islands i n t h e
best atlases represented conditions as they existed at the close of the eighteenth
century. Excellent as the work had been in its day, portions were erronmu8
o r obsolete, whlle many place names now in constant use had never gained
publication.

RECENT TOPOGRAPHIC AND CADASTRAL SURVEY
Immediately after the annexation of the Danish West Indies, the p r h e
necessity for a complete cadastral survey was obvious. The Coast and
Geodetic Survey, on account of the well-known refinement and accuraeg of Its
methods, was selected to undertake the important task. Less than flve months
after the inaugdration of the first American governor, Rear Admiral James H.
Oliver, he addressed the following letter to the Navy Department :
NAVALOUVIORNMINT, Tnm VIRGIN I S L A N D S O F TRBl U N l T l b D 8TATm8,

take this work at aa early a date a8 practicable.

At1 necesleary pmllminaries having been satlsfactorily arranged, on January
24, 1918, the Superintendent (now Director), Col. E). Lester Jones, ordered
Lleut. 0.W. Swainson, junior hydrographic and geodetic engineer, as chief
of party, to proceed to St. Thomas, consult with the naval government, and
undertake a complete detailed topographic survey on a scale of 1:10,000. A
trianguTatfon scheme was extended from points already established cn Culehrn.
Vieques, and Savana Islands, to cover St. Thomas, St. John, and St. Oroix, and
by arrangement with the Crown representative, to the nearest British Virgin
Islands, for future determination of the international boundary. Base lines
were measured on St. Thomas and St. Croix. Magnetic observations were
taken on St. Thomas, St. Croix, and Vieques. Fourteen plane-table sheets
were surveyed and plotted between 1918 and 1921, the last two sheets hy Lieut.
Eoline R. Hand, hydrographic and geodetic engineer. Estate boundaries on
St Croix were nearly all recovered and delimited. Provision was made
for subsequent hydrographic and wire-drag surveys, already approaching
completion.

The topographic sheets and other fleld records, particularly the descriptive
reports, contained, amid their abundance of geographic information, lists of unfmblished place names ; some newly proposed, others locally used, but of dubious
orthography ; still others, published with contradictory spellings ; not to speak
of different names for the same feature. Cartographers were confronted with
all sorts of quandaries.
These diversifled cases were referred to the author, a s a member of the
Unlted States Ueographic Board, for investigation and report, in connection
with- the review of the fleld records, the editing of the Porto Rico and Virgin
Islands Coast Pilot, the veriflcation of maps and charts in the drafting section,
and frequent requests for consultation on special cartographic prohlems, coming
from other branches of the service and other departments.
Scientific and linguistic research, in compliance with these various requirements, was carried on desultorily for two or three years, amid other
pressing duties, the wealth of carded data stendily accumulating. Interviews
were held with well-informed residents of the islands, and a voluminous correspondence conducted. During 1022 duplicate questionnaires, with lists of
numb&ed names, were sent to the governor of the islands and to several notctl
scholars.' A constant quest wns mnintained for originnl duthorltieo and source

PARTICIPATION BY THE UNITED STATES GEOGRAPHIC BOARD
It is the function of the United States Geographic Board to establish uniform usage in regard to geographic nomenclature and orthography throughout
the executive departments of the Government, and particularly upon the maps
and charts issued by the various departments and bureaus.. All unsettled
questions concerning geographic names which arise in the departments are to
be referred to the board and its decisions to be accepted by them as the standard authority in such matters. The board has the additional duty of determining, changing, and Axing place names within the United States and insular
possessions, and all names suggested for any place by any officer or employee
of the Government are required to be referred to said board for it6 approval
before publication.
Since the board does not attempt the impossible task of passing on the
myriads of names published by other competent authority or the vaster number in local use, the field for gazetteers of particular regions is left unoccupied.
The late secretary of the board and others urged the publication of our entire
collection of Virgin Islands names, to serve as a standard of reference, the
nucleus of a future geographic encyclopedia of this region.
NO funds for publication were available, however, until March, 1924, when
the MS. was rapidly typed, and two weeks later, April 2, 1924, exhibited at
the monthly meeting of the board, with a cordial invitation for constrpctive
criticism ; marked sheets, later replaced by cards, being flled for that purpose
with the secretary.
All names herein, whether of major or minor importance, not otherwise
sufficiently established, e. g., by prior publication or local use, have been referred
to the United States Geographic hoard for action, accompanied with ample
data; and decisions were obtained on all dubious cases of any moment. The
just-lasued "Index to the Fifth Report and Supplement (1830-23) of the
United States Geographic Board, 1924," lists 225 Barnes, adopted or rejected
agreeably to data and recommendations prepared by the Coast and Geodetic
Survey, for entry in the present work. Pursuant to a motion adopted April
2. 1924, a special meeting was culled in June, to consider all remaining cases
affecting this work, and requiring decision.
A certain number of relatively unimportant " last-moment " queMions, such
as assigning the name of a mill to its hill, an estate to its bay, etc., being
obviouely implied in any case, were canceled, in the absence of further action
by the board during the few weeks remaining ere the close of the fiscal year.
However, every established name discoverable as the result of exhaustive reMarch will be found herein, with citations of competent authorities.
SPECIAL APPLICATION OF GEOGRAPHIC B O A R D RULES
In regard to names published by recognized authorfty, or in good local use.
It is necessary only to conform to the established policy and guidlng pripclphs

-- 8 of 215 -
GEOGRAPHIC DIOTIONARY OF THE VIRUIN ISLANDS 5
enunciated by the Geographic Board (see Its Fifth Report, pp. 11-22) ; which,
in so f a r as applicable to the present work, may be thus summarized:
I' The possessive form should be avoided, whenever it can be done without
destroying the euphony of the name or changing its descriptive application."
The nominative singular should be accorded preference. Citing possessive or
plural forms when found does not b p l y their approval. Yet the rule is sometimes carried too far and should be set aside whenever the possessive indieates
actual ownership or is otherwise essential to the grammatical construction,
in which event substitution of the plural is a solecism.
" Discontinue the use of hyphens in connecting parts of names " and " simplify names coiiaisting of more than one word by their combination into
one word." This bureau strongly commends simpliflcation. The ideal in geographical nomenclature is n single name for a single feature. When the
elements of a compound name are monosyllabic, the above rule is applied
with ease, In this region the names generally are found to have been already
consolidated into one word, by the peculiarity of the Danish idiom, which
favors even the joining of the specific proper name with,its "generic appellative " (featural term), these, however, being regularly separated by the
English idiom. Hence, decompounding a t times is not only requisite, but may
preserve a useful distinction. E. g., English '' Cane Bay," Danish " Canebay,"
employed indiscriminately for a body of water and an estate; but under the
rule cited and interpretative resolutions, these two features have been distinguished in the order named. However, the board itself has not always been
consistent, and this work can not pretend to superiority in that respect, but
yields much to custom.
Religiously observed, in English or anglicized names; but, in those retaining their foreign
identity, mutilation obscures both the name and its meaning. Danish " 0 "
and ''8''nre distinct letters of the alphabet, with different sounds, as are
the Spanish '' N " und " R."
" Geographic names, like personal names, should not be translated." Iafractions have been frequent and lamentable, producing a monotonous Iteration
of commonplace names, whereas their retention in the original language used
by discoverers or residents would have preserved at once historic associations
and even more useful geographic distinctions. Only when there is involved a
slight change of form, with none in meuning, would it be pedantic not to
use the vernacular. E. g., " Lemon " (not " Limon "), " Whitesand " (not
" Hviide Sand ").
" I t is desirable to avoid the use of diacritic characters."
FORM AND ARRANGEMENT OF THE NOTICES
I n recording the names in this work certain minor departures have been
made from previous custom. When the proper name can not retain its
specific application if deprived of its generic term, this last is construed to
be an inseparable part of the name, which, therefore, is given in full, just
as it would appear on the map. E. g., " St. Thomas Harbor ;" not I' St. Thomas ;
Harbor ;" I' Water Island ;" not " Water ; Island." On the other hand, when
the name can stand alone, with Its generic term implied or understood, the
latter is relegated to be the flrst word in the descrlptlon. E. g., " St. Croix ;
Island ;" '' Maho ; Quarter ;" " Mafolle ; Estate."
The abundance of alternative names and variant spellings in a polyglot community is disconcertlng ; and, in the descriptions, association must guide the
choice of names to be mentioned. Special effort was made to clear up con
-- 9 of 215 -
6 U. S. COAST A N D GEODETIC SURVEY
fusion by assembling all the synonyms after the adopted name; with copious
cross references to supply additional information in regard -to each alternative.
The alphabetic arrangement is determined, not by the first word alone, but
by t h e order of letters in the entire name as f a r as the terminating semicolon.
This w u r e s the sequence of names, identically lettered, though differently
compounded ; of which there are many instances.
The Danish vowels A3 and $3, though in that language at the end of the
alphabet, are here entered with A and 0,where the English reader would
naturally look for them.
In capitalization and compounding in the text, the author has deferred to
present departmental usage.
The estates on St. Thomas and St. John were nearly all established during
the eighteenth century ;stherefore, may be accepted a s of equal antiquity. A
few still flourish: some have been abandoned within the memory of those
now living ; some a r e in ruins, interesting or vanishing ; others a r e but a tradition, verging on oblivion. But they remain equal, in not having been superseded, and in leaving to otherwise uenoted hills the heritage of their names.
Even where the object has perished, the name survives, for the spot.
I n a region of old European culture such as this, with its history, traditions,
and ruins, it becomes a mooted question whether or not any name ought to
be classified as entirely obsolete. The probability of i t s use on a modern English map, while the readiest test, can not be relied on too implicitly. In many
cases no two persons of good judgment would be likely to agree. Names indictated as obsolete, therefore, are not necessarily to be discarded.
T E R M S REFERRING T O T H E E S T A T E S
The word " plantage " occurs constantly on early maps fn several languages,
in histories, and in the public judicial records, as a portion of the name of the
original estates. E. g., ' I John de Windt's Plantage" Inter was known as
Bethlehem Estate." " Plantage " is used regularly with the name of the
original patentee, proprietor, or planter. After some hesitation, it seemed well
to leave it unchanged, when thus used.
Plantagegrund " (plural " Plantagegrunde ") was and is the offlcial term
applied to the estate tracts, herein usually referred to simply as '' tracts "
or by number. They were designated by serial numbers, beginning with "1,"
and running consecutively ( I ' boustrophedon," back and forth) through each of
t h e quarters. Each tract measured 2,000 by 3,000 Danish feet (equivalent to
2,059.378 by 3,089.067 English feet) i n extent; or, in area, 150 Danish acres,
each 100 alen ,square, covering 40,000 Danish square feet, and equivalent to
slightly more than 146.04 English acres, each covering 43,500 English square
feet.
The '' Plantegegrunde " (estate tracts) were disposed of in units, multiples,
o r submultiples, to colonists, whow family names in many instances a r e still
associated with the present estates. The consideration for the ground in each
case was merely a n annual rental of one turkey, deliverable to the governor
for his annual public banquet.
The English term 'I plantation " never gained vogue. The early '' plantages "
came to be known instead as "estates," which term is now used exclusively.
"Estate," however, has a twofold sense: First, as signifying the land helonging to the plantation: second, the "ma~ise"or plantation house, in St.
Croix called "Estate house," or "the great houae;' each with its environing
I' estate-village."
Care was taken 80 to word the context as to indicate clearly the sense In
which each of the foregoing terms was employed.
See note under "St. Croix."

DEFINITIONS AND DESCRIPTIONS
A systematic scheme was adopted for the arrangement and wording of the
definitions of the various classes of names. The design w a s to state the
essential characteristics and dimensions of each feature, giving a comprehensive description with the utmost conciseness compatible with clearness,
and locating each place with such definiteness as would enable the cartographer
to inscribe the name in its appropriate place, with full assumnce. Acknowledgment is made for valuable suggestions contained in several papers in the
" Atti " of the International Congress of Geography, held a t Rome in 1013.
Altitudes of hills in feet are from the recent determlnatione by the Coast
and Geodetic Survey. Geographic positions, from the same source, being fresh
and available information of potential value to surveyors and geographers, me
noted for absolute location.'" Vor the convenience of m:iriners. the widths of
bays between their named entrnnce points are stated in yards rnther than
meters, as first Intended. Areas of all the islands, cays. and larger rocks a r e
expressRl in English squnre measure, from the author's computations with a
Coradi planimeter.
Ulinnges of the estates nt vnfious periods a r e noted, to an extent suflicient
to enable the geographer to account for apparent discrepanciep between maps
of different dates. The rondcnsetl notes on names and limits of estates at
tliffcrent periods will be found sufflcicnt to reconstruct same by the aid of
Oxholm's maps.
Due mention is made of outstanding geogrnphical, historical, or scenic facts,
for which certnin places happen to be celebrated.
The origin and meaning of the names, their successlve changes of form and
application, a r e accounted for, along with t h e variant spelllngs, alternatives,
and linguistic equivalents, each of thesc~serving also as a cross reference to
its separate entry in the alphabetic nrrangement, where may be found additional information concerning the origin and use of that special name.
Citations of authorities, in the original notes very abundant, have been curtailed to a minimum; retaining only the earliest, least acceestble. or most
authentic references. When all ape:, any is superfluous.
The descriptive text often represents phraseology interwoven nnd amalgamated from the most diverse sources in half a dozen languages, with all nvailable fresh material. The best enrly map of St. Thomas was in Hollnnd
Dutch, the language also of the colonists of Rt. J o h n ; though the original
survey of the latter was in Danish, ns was- thnt of St. Croix, first settled by
the French, who wrote much on Caribbenn history; the most comprehensive
geographical descriptions a r e in German and Diinish ; the most satisfactory
hydrographic descriptions in English and Spanish : Government reports and
maps again in Danish: all combined into a coherent whole by the aid of the
recent American survey.

express their lively interest In the work; among whom should he mentloncd the
historinn, Dr. Waldemnr Wewtergnarcl ; Mr. E'rzinn T,iintl, who Iwrsonallg rcxrised
all doubtful names ; Mr. Axel Holst, a constant and valued correspondent;

Every human production has some inevitable shortcomings, and it i s too
much to hope this shall prove an exception. Being the first attempt of the
kfnd in this fleld, as well a s a flrst edition, occasional errors doubtless have
escaped detection, despite painstaking scrutiny. Nevertheless, it is believed by
those whose opinion merits respect that the present '' special publication '' presents in condensed form a sutacient amount 61 geographic information to
render it useful to any student of the science or the region. The work is
therefore offered a s a modest contribution to scientific knowledge.

GENERAL DESCRIPTION
Virgin Islands; Croup, in its entirety consisting of about 100 amall Islands
and cays with many outcropping rock ledges, in the West Indies, west of
Anegada Passage, at the northeast turn of the Caribbean Sea, which the
islands named there divide from the Atlantic Ocean. St. Croix being
geographically nbt included, the Virgin Islands properly so called occupy a
q a c e about 100 mfles long by 20 miles wide, ranging bettveen latitudes
18' 05' and 18" 46' north, and between longitudes 64" 12' and 65' 38' west.
The total area is given as 465 square mtles. On :iccount of the wmll size
of the islands, they are classed with the Leeward chain of the Caribbee
Islands ; although they form a geographical unit with Porto Rico, which is
counted with the Greater Antilles. Porto Rico, Vieques and Culebra, St.
Thomas and St. John, and the British Virgin Islands to Anegada, inclusive,
all rise out of the sea from a great shelf, denominated "Virgin Bank,"
extending through 3%' of longitude, the rim rising sheer from depths of
hundreds o r thousands of fathoms to depths of only 15 or 20 fathoms.
Between the Virgin Bank and St. Crois, only 40 mtles south, is a trough
with depths of 2 or 3 miles. Exposure to the Atlantic swell and the ramiflcations of the tidal waves generate extraordinary phenomena, rolling bib
lows often lashing the shore in calm weather.
Virgin Rank is 85 miles long, from 24 to 32 miles wide, and extends
eastward with a northerly trend from Porto Rico to beyond Anegnda.
North of the bank the Atlantic Ocean bed plunFpes in a steep dWliVfty for
100 miles to the trough of the Porto Rico ravine, nearly 600 miles long,
and with a deep at the western end of 4,660 fathoms. On t h e east the
bank enda at Anegnda Passage, 65 miles wide, leading into Caribbee Basin,
the western portion of which is known as St. Croix Deep, with 8 maximum
reported depth of 3,452 fathoinn. Between these viist abysses a mighty
submarine mountain rises steeply; till, within a few hundred fathoms of
the surface, it is crowned by a n almost perpendicular cllff, the brow of
which is the rim of a mesalike submerged plateau. This plateau is the
Virgin Bank. Thte face of the cliff is girdled by the 100-fathom curve;
depths along the brow range normally between 28 and 34 fathoms; but,
especially along its southern edge a coral reef parapet h a s been bullt up
to a least ascert:iined depth of 11 fathoms. This parapet, though only
about 200 yards wide, stretches from the eastermost point of the blink
southwestward unbroken for 55 miles and less continuously to Vieques.
Geologically the Vlrgin Islands a r e of volcanic formation ; the limestone,
serpentine, and igneous outcrops, with coral accretion% resting on a mass
of hard, bluish granite porphyry. St. Thomas Harbor is a n ancient crater.
Hill insists that the foundation rocks are Paleozoic, covered by great
masses of Cretaceous and Tertiary conglomerate and clay, derived from the
now vanished geologic Atlantis; these masses in turn veneered by the
mantle of oceanic chalky-white limestones; these last fringed by a n attenuated border of coral-reef rock. The older rocks described are penetrated
by dikes of ancient volcanic material.

U. S. COAST AND QEODETIC SURVEY
The vegetation of the Virgin Islands is naturally divisible into six
regional and generic sections, viz : marine a l g z and seaweed : shore plants ;
thicket ( K r a t ) ; forest (Skov) ; stream plants; and finally, cultivated
plants.
Many of the islands are rocky o r sandy and barren. Less than a third of
the group i s under cultivation. The products include sugar, molasses, rum,
coffee, maize, vegetables, fruits, ginger, pimento, turmeric, indigo, cotton,
tobacco, bay oil, and salt. Guinea grass flourishes on the hillsides,.aEording excellent pasturage. Forests, though few, contain mahogany, nut, and
other useful trees.' The coast waters teem with numerous species of food
fish. Total population, about 40,000.
The climate is warm, equable, agreeable, healthful, characterized by two
wet and two dry seasons. The North Atlantic trade wind blows steadily
from north-northeast for three-fourths of the year, the breeze becoming
brisk about 4 o'clock each morning and afternoon. Onc hundred and thirty
destructive hurricanes are recorded, with wind velocity as high as 150 miles
a n hour. The islands lie directly in the mean track of hurricanes. These
occur most frequently between August and October. Earth tremors are
frequent, but never severe.
The group was discovered by Columbus on his second voyage. November
14, 1493, h e anchored at the entrance of the bay just west of Saltriver
Point, 8t. Croix, to obtain fresh water, and had a skirmish with the natives,
who called the island Agay.
The long line of bold hilltops northward, as they emerged over the horizon,
would convey the impression of innumerable little islands, while " the long
procession of white, surf-beaten fringing reefs appears to have suggested
a fanciful resemblance to the 11,OOO British maidens of the St. Ursula
legend." Hence the Spanish name " Las Islas de las Vlrgenes," or simply
I' Las Vlergenes " ; French, " Les Vierges " or " lies des Vierges " ; Danish,
Jomfruprerne '' ; German, '' Jungfern-Inseln." The nunies of the three
larger islands were bestowed by Columbus himself. The smaller cuye were
named by the buccaneers.
The view 0 4 the islands from shipboard is most picturesque. The sky
line, broken by multitudinous dry gullies and little coves, reminded
Kingsley of the Auvergne Mountains : " Their water line has been exposed
t o the gnawing of the sea a t the present level, and evcrywlierc the cliffs
are freshly broken, toppIing down in dust and bowlders, and leaving detached stacks and skerries. Most beautiful meanwhile are the winding
channels of blue water, like landlocked lakes, which p a r t the Virgins from
each other; a n d beautiful the white triangular sails of the canoe-rigged
craft, which bent up and down them through strong currents and cockling
seas. The clear air, the still, soft outline, the rich yet delicate coloring,
stir u p a sense of purity and freshness, and peace and cheerfulness, such
as Fe stirred up by certain views of the Mediterranean and its shores."
The northeast half of the group has belonged to the British since 1666,
when they colonized Tortola. T h e other larger islands are Anegada, Virgin
Gorda, Jost Vandyke, Peter, Norman, Cooper, Ginger, Salt, Beef, Cumanoe,
Guano, Great Thatch, Great Tobago, etc.; altogether about 32; area, 58
1 Frults are abundant, often growlng spontaneously, such as cashew, cherry, cocoa,
calabaah, lime, meswi, murfrta, pineapple. tamarind. The following plants are worthy
of note: Junnlne avoado, mamey, eugenia, mursop, waterlemon, mango, custanlspple.
guava, umbrellatree, indigoberry, rockmyrtle, edenwwd, magrape, banana, m pun ti ti, orange,
kenep8; among other prQductions.

All the remainder of the group belongs to the United Stntes. The former
Spanish Virgin Islands were acquired in 1898, the former Danish Virgin
Islands in 1917. The demarcation line between the British and American
sections runs from the north between Little Tobago and Little Hans Lollik ;
thence through the Narrows between Great Thatch and St. J o h n ; thence
around east end of St. John, through Flanagan Passage between Flanagan
Island and Pelican Island, and south to the open sea.
Virgin Passage separates the former Danish Virgin Islands, including St'.
Thomas and St. John, with 62 smaller cays, from the former Spanish
Virgin Islands on the west, including Culebra and Vieques, administratively
attached to Porto Rico.
Virgin I s l a n d s of the United States: An arbitrnry administrative nanie,

the United States, but acquired from Spain with Porto Rico in 3898; while

last is geogrnphicRlly no part of the Virgin Islnnds group.

earliest foreign port to salute the flag of the young American Republic,
now enjoys the protection of that same flag, and is the easternmost outpost
over which i t floats.'
Under a n Act of Congress, approved March 3, 1917, known as the
Organic Act, all military, civil, and judicial powers necessary to govern
the islands were vested in a Governor, appointed by the President of the
United States, by and with the advice of the Senate; and all laws, in 80 f a r
as compatible with the changed sovereignty, as set forth in the Danish
code of laws dated April 6, 1906, were continued in force and effect until
Congress shall provide otherwise. T a x and customs laws were likewise
to remain in effect. Danish citizens residing in the islands at the time the
treaty was entered into were permitted to retain their Danish citizenship
by making declaration before a court of record to pre8erve such citizenship,
in default of which they were held to have renounced it, and to have accepted citizenship in the United States.
March 31, 1917, the Dannebrog was lowered, and the Stars and
Stripes were hoisted. Commander Edwin T. Pollock, of U. 8. S. H&ncock,
read the proclamation of President Wilson, and took formal possession
of the territory, announcing that it would thereafter be known as the
'' Virgin Islands of the United Statea of America."
,June, 1917, naval government letterheads bore the legend, ''The Virgin
Islands of the TJnited States." September, 1919, the Acting Secretary of
the Navy referred to the islands i.n official correspondence by the same
designation. April, 1922, the Acting Chief Of Naval Operatlons stated t h a t
the governor's proper title in, " Governor, Virgin Islands of the United
Sttltes." Navy Department usage was thus established. The Statesman's
Yerirhook, as well a s the Geographic Board Index of Decisions, 1924,
conform to this usage.
Between Decemher 24, 1917, and January 12, 1918, a census taken by
the United States showed the total population of the 6 inhabited islands,
via, St. Thomas, Hassel Island, Water Island, Lovango Cay. St. John, St.
Croix, and Protestant Cay, to be 26,051; of whom 1922 (7% per cent)
were white.
The population in 1828 had been 46,000; in 1841, 41,000; in 1890, 32,000.
Education free arid school attendance compulsory between ages of 7
and 15, under school law of Jnly 1, 1921. Ninety-one teachers and 2.681
pupits are enrolled in the 21 puhlic schools, 2 night schools, and 3 junior
high schoole; attendance 88.8 per cent : 14 good private schools.
The islands are administered by Navy officers, appointed by the President,
with the designation, "Governor of the Virgin Islands (of the United
States), and Commandant of the Naval Station, St. Thoma8, V. I." The
Qovernor's cabinet is composed of the Government Secretary and the
heads of the sfx departments of Health, Judiciary, Public Works, Education, Supply, and Police and Fire, located a t St. Thmna8, and represented
at Chrlstiansted by a DispnQching Secretary.

QEOORAPHIC DICTIONARY OF THE VIRGIN ISLANDS 23
S. E. Kittelle, 1922; Capt. Henry H. Hough, 1923, and Capt. Philip

Administratively, the islands are divided into two municipalities, the
legislative functions of each being vested in a colonial council, viz., (1)
St. Thomas and St. John, including 62 adjacent cays, with a council of
15 members, 11 of whom are elected and 4 appointed; (2) St. Crois, including 3 adjacent cays, with a council of 18 members, 13 being elected and
5 appointed. Tenure, 4 years. Suffrtige qualifications : Unblemished character, 5 years' residence, age 25, annuiil incwme of 5300. Each municipality
has also a judge end government attorney. The three principal islands
are further subdivided into quarters, to which the cays are attached; and
the quarters into estates. Four hundred and thirty farms ere under
cultivation, occupying 69,892 acres.

The Quebec Steamship Co. maintains a regular fortnightly passenger and
freight service from New York to St. Thomas end 8t. Croix. The Clyde
Steamship Co. (ttaporel Line) operates a semimonthly freight service from
New York to St. Thomas, St. Croix, and the Leeward Islands. The New
York 8: Porto Rico Stearnship Co. arid the Red " D " line operate regular
service from New York to Snn Juan, P. E., whence schooner service may be
had to ports in the Virgin Islands. Each municipality has a post offlce
and a cnble omce and telephone systems. Tiit! naval station, Yt. Thonius,
operates a powerful radio station.

Calvary Bay; 370 yards wide, 820 yards long, sheltered, in southwest part of
St. John I s l a n d . 4 . B. Long known by the hybrid English-Spanish name,
"Great Cruz Bay." St. John having been settled by the Dutch, the original name doubtless was " Groot-Kruis Ray " ; or Danish, '' StoreKrys ''
or " Store-Kors " Bay. Oldendorp and Dewitz give it as '' Gross-Cruxbay ''
(Ger.) ; Oxholm, as " StoreCruytz," and the Derrotero, 'I Bahia Grande
de la Cruz." Since Crue Bay has come to refer to the post omce on Little
Crue Bay, Q. v., to avoid confusion, the name of the bay has been conformed
to that of the post oflice, dropping altogether the name "Great Cruz."
Since this last refers to the " Great Cross of Calvary," the name I' Calvary
Bay " was favored by several well-informed authorities and indorsed by
the late President Harding, in compliment to Calvary Baptist Church,
of Washington, D. C., of which he was an honored attendant.

tlanstsed, Christianstedt, Christlanstadt, Hristiansted, but generally known

Christiansted Harbor; Main basin 1% miles wide, sheltered on north by
Long Reef. Situated 10 miles east of Ham Bluff, 794 miles west of East
Point, St. Croix. In Danish called, " Christlanssted Havn " or '' Christiansteds H a m " ; popularly, '' Bassin Harbor " ; from the early French name
(1761), Le Bassin, or Bassin, meaning a harbor basin. Described in
"Port0 Rico and Virgin Islands Coast Pilot," pp. 137-8. The inter-island
packet, Vigilant, built in 1802 for a Danlsh privateer, defeated a Spanish
gunboat, turned slaveship, sank in the 1916 hurricane, was raised and
retimbered. In 1921 a preliminary examination of the harbor was made
by the United States Corps of Engineers.

for affjncent cays, makiog a total of $2.24 square miles. The island is
elongatM in form, atretching east-northeast west-southwest ;extreme length,
23 nlfles; maximunl width, 6 miles. The hlghea summit, Mount Eagle,
h&s an altitude of 1,169 feel!. The island is phpiographically divided into
thPee regions: West Oblong, Neck and East Triangle. There are two
hill systems, built of Bluebeach; Central Slope and the Neck, of Marlstone.
St. Croix is separated from St. Thomas by a deep of 1,100 to 2,450
fathoma. It rises steepto 600 to 800 fathoms from sea on northwest and
west. Edge of submarine shelf, equal to island in area, is marked by 10
and 100 fathom ctwves, closely parallel. Northeast of the Neck, the shelf
makes seaward; averaging 3 miles ofibhore, north and south, at Eastend ; prolongrrtion, called Lang Bank, continuing east-northeast 10 miles.
Shoals along entire south coast for 2 or 3 miles oflshore. Climate warm
but congenial. Mean temperature for 28 years ranges from 78.3' F. in
February to 82" in August. Normeast tradewind blows most of year;
rainy season, August to December ; average precipitation, 47.56 inches.
St. Croix has belonged successively
to the Caribs, Spanish, English, French, Dutch, Danes, and Americans.
The island was discovered by Columbus on his aecmd voyage, November 14,
1493. H e anchored, it i6 reported, near Saltriver Point to obtain water,
and met Indians stmilar to the Carib1 of (fuadeloupe, with Arawak captives from Porto Rico. The natives called the island, Ayai or Agay. Here
was fought the drat pitched battle in America between the red natives and
the white invaders. The savage Caribs of b o t h sexes desperately attacked
the armorclad Spaniards, killing one with a poisoned arrow. Later, the
slaveraiders from Hispanlola seized mRny natives, and a proclamation by
Charles V ordered their extermination. In 60 years they had vanished.
The Earl of Cumberland reported St. Croix a wilderness. Two colonies, one of Dutch, the other of French settlers, arrived in 1625 and
were joined by French refugees. In 1646 the population was over 800.
After a bloody clash with the English, in 1649, the Dutch retired to St.
Eustatius and St. Martin. In 1660,1,200 Spaniards from Porto Rico in turn
massacred the English, t h e remnant escaping to St. Kitts and Bermuda.
The French governor, De Poincy, in 1651, sent De Vaugalan with 160 men
from et. Ritts to evict the Spaniards. To clear out the fatal miasma, the
French flred the dense forests, retreated aboarcl their ships, to observe the
va5t conflkgration, lasting several months, then returned, planted the
ground, and prosp&ed greatly. The soil was amazingly fertile, yielding
abundant crops of tobacco, cotton, anatto, indigo, and sugar. Within 11
years the white population numbered 822.'
The history is varied and evatful.

BIOdRAPHICAL SKETCH OF ADMIRAL PILLSBrfRY
j o h n Elliott Pillsbury was born at Lowell, Mass., on December 15, 1846;
the son of John Gilman and Elizabeth Wlmble Pillsbury. He graduated from
the United States Naval Academy in 1867; was promoted to the rank of
lilnslgn In 1868, master In 1870, and lieutenant in 1872. h 1873 he married
Miss Florence Greenwood Aitchison, of Portland, Me. He w a s exceptionally
devoted to his home and family.
Lieutenant Pillsbury served on various duties and stations, including one
year in the Hydrographic Offlce of the Navy and ten years In the U. 8. Coast
Survey. I n this bureau, flrst as hydrographic inspector, he summarized the
results of deep-sea soundings obtained by the Steamer Blake in lsSO-lSS3~
to determine the configuration of the west Atlantic Ocean bed; and later he wag
Pesponsible for the final verification and revision of important portions of
t h e Atlantic Coast Pilot.
The plans formulated by Professor Bache, Superintendent of the U. 5.
Coast Survey between 1844 and 1866, providing for a systematic investlgation
of the Gulf btrepm, recelved a great impetus toward their complete accomplishment, when, in 1884, Superintendent Hilgard assigned Lieutenant Pillsbury to the command of the survey steamer Blake, which, for the ensuing
seven years, was engaged 14 the investigation of the Gulf Stream currents from
their source In the Caribbean along their course to beyond the Newfoundland
Banka By a n original devlce, Pillsbury anchored the Blake in the stream
itself, over depths of from % to 2% miles, and secured observations at various
depths below the surface, employing ingenious instruments and apparatus of
hls own invention. He deflnitely located the a?& of the stream in the Florida
Straits and ofp Cape Hatteras, besides ascertaining many important facts pertaining to its origin and causes, temperature and salinity, direction and
loclty and position, and the tidal e f f w in it of the sun and
moon. His contributions t o a knowledge of the immutable laws controlling its
movements brought withln the range of scientific prediction the puzzling
fluctuation8 of this vast oceanic river, which pours through the Florida
&rafts 90 billlon tons of warm water every hour.
J u l y 1, 1802,after 20 years of meritorious service i n the grade of lieutenant,
h e was advanced to that of lieutenant commander, and August 10,1898, to that
of commarider. Daring the Spanish-American War, he commanded the dynamite cruiser Vesuviua at the slege of Santiago de Cuba. Thereafter he was in
charge of the equipment department at the Boston Navy Yard, and In 1901-2
W a s again afloat, commanding the U. 5. 9. Prairie. He was next detailed to
duty in Washington as a member of the General Board and, assistant to the
Chief of the Bureau of Navigation. November 10, 1902, he was promoted to
the rank of captain. I n 1905 he became t h e Chief of Staff of the North Atlantic
Fleet, and in 1908-9 served as Chief of the Bureau of Navigation. July 4,
1908, h e was made a rear admiral, and on December 15 was retired. In
July, 1909, he was relieved from active duty.
Admiral Pillsbury was a fellow of the American Association for the Advancement of Sclence, and a member of the American Academy of Arts and
209

210 U. 6. COAST AND GEODETIC SURVEY
Sciences. I n 1909, he accepted a place on the Eoard of Alanngers of the
National Geographic Society, was vice president of the society from 1915
until 1919, was elected as its president in April, 1919, and served until his
death. His Washington residelice was at 1755 Q Street, NW. He died, at
the age of 73 years, on December 30, 1919.
Among Admiral Pillsbury's bersonnl characteristics. none was more notable
than his devOtion to duty. His painstaking thorougliness in study and research insured in advance the complete anb able performance' of any task entrugted to him. H;'s own enthusiasm made him ap infjlpiring leader; able to
maintain excellent discipline, without austerity. His superb intellectual gif@
had made him master of a vast store of knowledge. Pet, desqite his personal
accomplishments and scientific achievements, he remained. till the last, sinm
lorly modest and retiring.
